# Memory-frames experiment

Fifteen ways of holding a memory over a decade of turns, compared: what each stores, how each decides what to keep, what each answers and what each costs. §The structures compared is that comparison on one page, and §What would be chosen today is what it comes to. The design this project ships is one of the fifteen and is the baseline the rest are read against; four more come from published papers and §Where the published designs came from says what was searched, what was ported and what each design needed that the seam withholds. The seam is what lets structurally different designs sit beside one another at all.

## The structures compared

Every structure measured, at the last year of the decade run and in the arm where a fact-carrying turn arrives already extracted. The last five rows are published designs ported behind the same seam and §Where the published designs came from says which paper each is and what it needed.

Three rows are not candidates. `attribute-declared` and `interval-declared` are ceilings: the write hands them the decision every other row has to make. `mem0-nearest` is the far end of a bracket rather than a design anyone proposes. A reader scanning for the best row should know which rows are not competing.

<!-- end generated -->

Three things are read off each answer: **recall**, the share of currently-true values present in the injected context, **stale returned**, whether any value that has stopped being true is present, and **units read**, how much of what the frame holds it touched to produce the answer, counted in the unit its own size is counted in. The six class columns are the six ways a fact behaves over a decade and §The workload defines each.

Recall is never reported without the stale figure beside it. A frame that answers everything by keeping everything posts the best recall in the table and the worst stale figure, and a reader ranking on recall alone would crown it. Units read counts what a structure touches and not how long that takes: this record measures no latency, and the count is not a proxy for one. What it says is whether answering one question costs a structure a lookup or a pass over everything it holds, which over a decade is the difference between a store that stays answerable and one that does not.

A size is one store read at one moment rather than a sample, so it carries no interval; every other figure in this record is a mean over the probes of that year with the 95% half-width those probes support. The other arm, in which nothing arrives extracted, is not in this table and moves these rows a long way; §What the comparison shows says how, and §The seam says why both arms are run.

Every question behind this table arrives already turned into an address, so these rows say what a structure holds and updates and nothing about what it finds. §Whether a structure can find what it holds is the run that withholds the address, and it moves the addressed rows further than either arm does.

## What would be chosen today

The programme moves from measuring to deciding after this round, so this is where the record says where it stands. Two of the three questions are answered by the evidence and the third is not.

**The structure is chosen: a fact is held at its address, and arity comes from the write.** Addressing removes the failure the shipped design has — supersession inferred from similarity — outright rather than improving it, and every arm that tried to recover arity from the stored triple failed or reproduced one of the degenerate ends. Among the stores that take a declaration, the plain addressed one answers exactly what the interval store answers while holding less and reading less, so the simpler one is the one to build. The interval store earns its extra bytes only if a question about an earlier year is ever asked, and nothing in this workload asks one.

**The direction of error is chosen: where a writeback must err, it errs toward join.** That is not a preference. A wrong join is repaired by the next correct replacement at that address and carries its damage for no checkpoints; a wrong replacement is repaired by nothing and carries it to the end of the span. The asymmetry is measured, it is structural, and stating it costs no code.

**What constraint changes.** Under a capacity that bites, the choice above stops being the thing that decides the outcome: every structure that holds facts answers about the same, and what separates them is the eviction rule rather than the store. That does not withdraw the choice — at the capacities a decade actually reaches, nothing evicts at all — but it narrows what the choice is worth. Two things it adds. A rule keyed on use is enough to decide a forgetting, which arity was not, so that decision does not have to move into the write. And a capacity bound on the shipped design makes its footprint grow rather than shrink, because what it evicts it archives; a structure that holds facts and nothing else does not have that property, and under a bound that difference is larger than any answer either of them gives.

**What this round adds to the structure.** The addressed store's weakness under the read-and-update criterion is that it brings back little beyond the address asked, so a turn's writeback sees little it could correct. That would be decisive if a fact had to be read before it could be fixed — and the constructed case says it does not, for this structure: the write computes its address and lands whether or not any read ever returned it. The same case says the opposite for the store that supersedes by similarity. So the criterion the user named separates these two designs further rather than closing the gap, and the choice already stated stands.

**The structure survives harder language and the writeback is where it bends.** An addressed store answers identically on plain, subordinated and packed sentences whenever the writeback is perfect, because what it holds is an address and a value; the store that answers by matching a question against text it kept loses about a quarter of its recall on subordinated sentences and most of one class on packed ones. So the shape of the language does not reach the chosen structure — it reaches the writeback standing in front of it, and it reaches the part of that writeback this section already named as the open question. What breaks first is the declaration: of the facts a model both finds and addresses correctly, the share whose operation it also gets right falls furthest of anything the extraction measures across the shapes. What breaks second is the address, which was never wrong on plain sentences and is wrong most often where a message carries three assertions. Neither failure could have appeared in the decade run, whose oracle never errs, and both land on exactly the two things the choice rests on — that a fact reaches its address, and that the write states arity. The choice stands; what this round adds is that the reliability of a declaration is a property of the sentence and not only of the model.

**The structure cannot find, and the finding has to live in front of it.** Every figure behind the choice above was produced with the question already turned into an address. Asked the same questions in words, every structure that holds facts at an address answers nothing at all, in the arm where it holds everything: there is nowhere to look when a question carries no key. That is not a score to be improved and no arity rule repairs it — it is the other face of what addressing bought, which is that identity is settled before the read rather than inferred during it. So the design that would be built is not one thing but three: a writeback that reads a sentence into an address, a store that holds an address and a value, and a read step that reads a question into an address. §Whether a structure can find what it holds measures the third — one model call a question, and behind it the store answers what it answers under the oracle. What survives is the simplicity claim about the store. What does not survive is any claim that the store on its own is a memory: two of the three parts read language, and the store between them does no finding whatever.

**The alternative is the design this record already measures, and it is a worse trade rather than an unmeasured one.** A store that ranks text against the question needs no step in front of it, and the same rows say what that buys and costs: it finds nearly as much of a person, it reads its whole index to do it, it returns a superseded value on most subjects, and its footprint is orders of magnitude larger because it keeps the sentences. Nor is its read path free of a call — it embeds the question before it ranks, which on the hosted embedder is a call per read (#435). So the comparison is not a call against no call; it is one call that returns an address against one call that returns a vector, and everything downstream of them differs. A design that wanted both would pay for both stores, which the byte columns price.

**A question spanning many addresses is where the choice starts to cost, and nothing in the structure decides it.** Asked what it knows about a person, an addressed store returns every address under that subject: no ranking, no threshold, and no budget either. Where the relevant set is larger than the read budget it returns it anyway, and at the tightest budget swept it already does on half the subjects for the arm that keeps every value. The only structure here that chooses what to leave out is the shipped one, and it chooses with the hybrid score's constants — the mechanism this programme disqualified when the question was arity. So the choice is between a structure that overflows and a ranking with constants nobody calibrated, and this round declines to put the second inside the first. Where an ordering would have to live, if one is wanted, is the read step that already reads the question: it knows what was asked, and a step that can turn a question into an address can turn it into fewer addresses. That is a design to specify and measure, not a constant to fit, and it is not in this record.

**The policy is not chosen, and the evidence cannot choose it.** Taking a real model's declarations recovers most of the coexistence an always-replacing store gives up, and pays for it with a superseded value on about one revised thread in five. Always replacing returns nothing stale and loses that coexistence permanently. Neither dominates. Which is cheaper depends on whether a fact that is lost costs more than a fact that is wrong, and no measurement in this record answers that — it is a property of what the agent is for, not of the structure. A reader looking for the row with the best numbers should be told that the best-scoring rule measured here is `attribute-cued`, which is disqualified for being a list that grows, and that it sits on the frontier rather than below it. The criterion has to be applied deliberately; it is not visible in the ranking.

**What the published designs change, and what they do not.** Four designs from the literature now sit in these tables, measured the same way, and the choice above stands. None of them answers more of the workload than the addressed store while returning less that is stale; the three that store the conversation rather than what it asserted answer a small fraction of it and freeze most of what they hold; the one that stores facts and updates them, `mem0`, differs from the recommendation in one decision and loses on that decision.

Two things they do change. The first is that the recommendation's advantage is now located precisely. `mem0` takes the same declaration from the same model and differs only in finding the memory it updates by similarity instead of computing where it is — and §What a similarity-located write has to separate shows that on this workload the value at the address a write lands on is no nearer that write than the nearest value somewhere else. So "hold a fact at its address" is not a convenience over searching for it; it is the difference between an update that lands and one that lands on something else. `memory-retrieval/` refuted similarity as a way of deciding *whether* two statements are about one fact; this round refutes it as a way of finding *which* memory a write is for, on the same workload and by a separate measurement.

The second is a figure where a published design is ahead and it is stated rather than buried. Driven by a real model's declarations, `mem0` answers more than `attribute-declared` and keeps `refined` where the addressed store loses a quarter of it. What buys that is that its updates rarely land, so the model's destroying errors mostly fail to destroy — and the price is a superseded value on nearly every revised thread, more than four times the addressed store's. It is the always-join end of the trade this section already declines to settle, reached by a structure rather than by a policy. It does not change the choice; it does say that the choice is between the same two failures whichever design one starts from.

**And one claim of an earlier round is now wrong.** §What a read cannot reach concluded that nothing freezes on this workload and that the workload was too small to produce the failure the criterion names. It was not the workload. Two of the published designs freeze most of what they hold from the first year, and the difference is that they store every turn while everything measured before stored what a turn asserted. The prediction that section made — that the frozen share turns positive once a store outruns what a decade of reads can carry — is confirmed, and the qualifier it attached to it is withdrawn.

What would be built today, then, is the addressed store with the operation stated by the writeback, erring toward join, and a read step in front of it that turns a question into an address. What is still open is what to do when the declaration is unreliable — a decision to take rather than a measurement to make, and one this round makes more pressing rather than easier, because it has now measured a sentence shape that makes the declaration less reliable. What is open beside it is what the read step should ask for when a question reaches more than fits.

## What the comparison shows

The rows fall into families, and what separates a family from the rest is larger than what separates two rows inside one.

**Addressing.** A fact is held at its address, `(subject, attribute)`. Writing to an address is how a value is replaced, so identity is settled outright and similarity appears nowhere in the write path: the mechanism `memory-retrieval/` refuted is not weakened here, it is absent. The frame keeps no sentence, no vector and no keyword index — an address and a value — and answers a question by going to one place rather than by filling a budget. Four arms differ in how many values one address holds and in nothing else: `attribute-single` keeps one and a write replaces it; `attribute-multi` keeps every value ever written; `attribute-learned` and `attribute-cued` are the two rules §The arity question states; `attribute-declared` is the ceiling.

**A stream, ranked at read time.** Three of the published designs decide nothing when a turn arrives: every observation becomes a record, and what separates them is what a read ranks on and whether anything is ever dropped. `generative-agents` drops nothing and ranks on recency, importance and relevance together; `reflection` is the same store with a synthesized record written over recent ones; `memorybank` is the same ranking with a forgetting curve removing what no read has returned lately. All three fill the shipped recall budget by ranking, which is the shipped design's read path under a different score — so their rows are where a reader sees what a score is worth once the store's shape is held constant.

**Searching for the memory to update, rather than computing where it is.** `mem0` is the only structure here that takes the write's operation and still has to find what the operation applies to. Every other structure that updates computes the address, and every structure that does not compute an address does not update at all. That makes the pair `mem0` and `attribute-declared` the one comparison in this record where arity is held constant and identity is the variable, and §What a similarity-located write has to separate is the evidence about whether the variable can be resolved on this workload at all.

**Why three of the ten post the same rows.** `attribute-single`, `interval` and `log` take the same arity decision — the newest value at an address is what is true there — in three structurally different stores. They therefore answer identically, class for class and year for year, and the tables below show that. It is the round's result rather than a copy-paste: the store shape does not change the answer, the arity rule does. `harness/measure_test.go` asserts the equality, so it fails if it stops being true. Where they differ is what they hold, what they read to answer, and what they can be asked, and those three columns are where this comparison lives.

**What these figures say.**

- **Addressing removes the baseline's failure outright and exposes a different one.** Every attribute arm answers `replaced` and `reverted` in full at every year, where the shipped structure returns a superseded value on most revised threads from the first replacement onward. Identity at an address is not a better inference than identity from similarity; it is no inference, so there is nothing left to be wrong.
- **An addressed structure is inert without an extractor.** In the `text` arm every attribute arm holds nothing and answers nothing at every year: there is nowhere to put a sentence it cannot address, and nowhere to look when a question cannot be addressed either. The shipped structure keeps a floor there and this one has none, which is the sharper form of the same dependency.
- **Holding facts rather than sentences costs orders of magnitude less than the shipped store, and injecting an address costs orders of magnitude less than filling a budget.** At the last year the attribute arms hold a comparable number of things to the shipped active index and store a small fraction of its bytes, because an address and a value are all they keep — no sentence, no vector, no keyword index. What they put in the context is a line or two rather than a filled token budget, and their recall is measured against that.
- **The whole distance between every self-deciding structure and a perfect one is arity.** `attribute-declared` answers every class at every year and returns a superseded value on nothing, because the write states what the store would otherwise have to infer. No arm here is short of it for any other reason: the addressing is the same, the workload is the same, and the gap is the one decision the seam withholds.
- **The store shape does not change the answer; the arity rule does.** `attribute-single`, `interval` and `log` are three different stores taking one arity decision, and their recall and stale figures are identical at every year and every class. Whatever else is chosen between an addressed store, a store of spans and a log with a projection, it is not chosen on what the memory answers.
- **What they can be asked and what they cost differ, and that is the whole of the comparison.** The interval store and the log each keep a row for every assertion and hold more than the plain addressed store, which keeps a row for every value that is still true. The log reads every event it has ever kept to answer one question and the addressed stores read one address, and the gap between those two columns widens every year while the answers stay equal.
- **What a structure stores separates the table further than how it decides.** Every structure that stores what a turn asserted answers most of what it is asked; every structure that stores the turn answers a small fraction of it, whatever it ranks on and whether or not it forgets. The gap between those two groups is an order of magnitude and it is wider than any gap inside either. The arity question, the destroying error and the eviction rule are all questions asked inside the first group; they do not arise in the second, because a structure holding sentences has nowhere to take those decisions.
- **The published designs' failures are not the failures this record has been cataloguing.** They do not decide arity wrongly — `generative-agents` answers nothing at all on `replaced`, because three sentences about one attribute sit in the store and a ranking has no notion of which is current. They do not evict wrongly — `memorybank` evicts on a curve written in days and a decade is thousands of them. And one of them fails in a way no structure invented here can: `mem0` is short on `carried`, a fact asserted once and never revised, because a write meant for one address ended a value at another.
- **A store of everything is unreachable before it is full.** `generative-agents` and `reflection` freeze four fifths and seven tenths of what they hold, and reach the active-index capacity inside a second decade where every fact-holding structure is twelve decades away. Both are consequences of storing the conversation rather than what it asserted, and the first arrives long before the second.
- **A structure that reads everything it holds does not survive a decade, whatever its recall says.** The shipped design ranks its whole active index on every recall, and the units it reads to answer one question rise with the store across the span. The log's projection rises the same way, for the same reason: it is derived rather than stored. `attribute-learned` is the third, and this is a cost #453 did not see — its rule reads every address it holds to decide how many values one of them returns, so answering one question touches all of them while every other addressed arm touches one.

## `shipped` — `internal/memory` as it runs

`internal/memory` as it runs: the SQLite store, hybrid search over embeddings and FTS, the one ranking function, supersession on insert, and the two passes `internal/gateway/queue.go` runs after a completed turn, threshold consolidation and tiered retention. `Recall` is what it puts in the context, bounded by the shipped token budget.

**What these figures say.**

- **The shipped structure holds a fact for the whole span and does not remove the one it replaced.** In the `oracle` arm, `carried` — asserted once in the first year and never mentioned again — is answered as often in the last year as in the first, within its interval, while the stale figure is high from the first replacement year onward and does not fall afterwards. Holding and updating are separate abilities, and this structure has the first without the second.
- **The value it never removed is the value it answers best.** `reverted` is the lowest class of all while the replacement is what is true, and rises to answering every thread in the year each returns to the value it started with — a value the store still held, because nothing had taken it out. `replaced`, where the superseded values have to go, falls across the span instead. A structure that never forgets is punished by a workload that changes its mind and rewarded by one that changes it back.
- **The writeback decides the outcome and the store barely enters it.** The same store, the same search and the same ranking function are orders of magnitude apart between the two arms. A turn arriving without an extracted fact is an episodic observation, its salience tier expires inside the retention window, and a span of years is a great many windows.
- **Even the current year's fact is gone by the year's end without the oracle.** `fresh` is asserted in the year it is asked, and is answered almost every time in the `oracle` arm and almost never in the `text` one. The retention window is measured in days and a checkpoint stands at the end of a year.
- **Both coexistence classes decay, and not together.** `parallel` and `refined` require the same thing — two simultaneously-true values of one attribute surviving — and differ only in how the second is worded. `carried` holds level across the span while both of them fall, and `refined` falls further from the year its second value arrives. Their intervals overlap, so the gap between the two wordings is something to widen the workload for rather than a separation this run makes.
- **What a decade costs on disk is not what it holds.** Stored bytes run orders of magnitude above content bytes at every checkpoint in both arms, and in the `text` arm they climb while the active index shrinks. The store never hard-deletes, so every turn ever observed is still paid for long after retention has taken it out of every search.

## `document` — one bounded document and no store

No store at all: one bounded document holding the most recent turns' text, rewritten each turn, dropping the oldest to stay inside a limit set to the shipped token budget so both frames put contexts of the same size in front of the model. It performs no retrieval and returns the document whatever the question. It is the floor a structure with a store has to beat to be buying anything.

**What these figures say.**

- **A structure with no store at all is at or above the shipped structure without its oracle, in every year.** The intervals overlap throughout, so this run does not separate the two. What it does say is that a store its own retention empties is worth no more than one bounded document of the same size.

## `attribute-single` — one value at an address

One value at `(subject, attribute)`, and a write replaces what is there. It is one end of the addressed family §What the comparison shows describes.

**What these figures say.**

- **It fails in the year the workload's shape predicts.** It answers everything until the year a second simultaneously-true value is asserted, then holds exactly one of the two on both coexistence classes, and its stale figure never leaves zero. That is not a failure of addressing. It is the arity decision taken at one end, showing up where the workload asks about it.

## `attribute-multi` — every value at an address

Every value ever written at `(subject, attribute)`, and a write never replaces. It is the other end of the addressed family §What the comparison shows describes.

**What these figures say.**

- **It fails in the opposite direction, in the year the workload's shape predicts.** It never loses a value and returns a superseded one on every revised thread from the first replacement year on. That is not a failure of addressing either. It is the arity decision taken at the other end, showing up where the workload asks about it.

## `attribute-learned` — arity read off the attribute's other addresses

Arity as a property of the attribute, learned from the attribute's own addresses: an attribute holds several values once any address under it has been written more than one, read off what the frame has observed rather than off a list of attributes written into it. Deciding at the read destroys nothing at the write, so what it holds is `attribute-multi`'s. It is the one candidate of the four §The arity question weighed that was built rather than rejected on paper.

**What these figures say.**

- **`attribute-learned` is `attribute-multi`**, row for row, year for year and class for class, and it pays `attribute-multi`'s storage to get there — a rule that decides at read time cannot have destroyed anything at write time.
- **It collapses, and §What the addresses look like says why.** Every attribute carries threads from every class, so the share of its addresses ever written a second value falls in one narrow band for all twelve of them. The generator assigns classes to subject-attribute pairs by shuffling every pair, so where inside that band an attribute lands is which threads it happened to be given and not a fact about the attribute — and a threshold set inside the band would separate attributes by that and nothing else. At the weakest threshold every attribute turns multi-valued the moment the first replacement lands, and the arm's rows are `attribute-multi`'s at every year and every class. At a majority threshold none of them ever turns, and it is `attribute-single`. The rule does not decide arity; the threshold does, and nothing calibrates the threshold.

## `attribute-cued` — arity read off the sentence's wording

One or several values at `(subject, attribute)`. A value joins the ones already at its address when the sentence carries a phrase that marks an addition, and replaces them otherwise. It is the rule §The arity question arrives at once the other candidates have been read off the workload: what separates the classes is the wording, and this is that written down.

**What these figures say.**

- **The best-scoring structure measured here is disqualified, and the figure that disqualifies it sits beside the figure that wins.** `attribute-cued` is the only arm that answers every replacement without ever returning a superseded value and answers `parallel` in full as well. It fails one class, `refined`, which asks for exactly what `parallel` asks for in different words. The bullet below states why that is a disqualification rather than a defect to fix.
- **It scores best of every arm measured here, on both figures at once, and it is disqualified.** Not because reading language is illegitimate — the writeback this project ships reads language and every model-driven design will — but because the rule is a list that grows. Nothing in it generates its next entry. The workload's narrowing statements mark an addition with a qualifier instead of a conjunction, the list has no conjunction to find, and they are read as replacements: `refined` halves while `parallel`, which is the same requirement worded with a conjunction, is answered in full. Adding qualifiers to the list would answer `refined` and answer nothing else. That the repair exists and is a clause is what disqualifies the rule, and the two class columns beside each other are where a reader checks it rather than taking it on trust.

## `attribute-declared` — arity stated by the write

**Not a candidate and not a structure.** It is the attribute store with arity supplied rather than decided: the write states whether the value replaces what is at its address or joins it, and the frame takes it. A frame reading that is not answering the arity question, it is assuming it answered. It is here as the ceiling every structure that has to decide for itself is read against, and it belongs in no ranking of designs — a reader scanning the rows for the best one has found a row that is not competing.

§What the comparison shows reads the distance between its row and every structure that has to decide arity for itself. What it costs when the declaration is wrong is its own study, because in production that declaration comes from a model reading a sentence: §What a wrong declaration costs sweeps the error rate and §Whether a write need destroy asks whether the damage can be made non-destructive.

## `interval` — a value with the span it was true for

A value is written with a start and is never removed. A replacement closes the open span instead of deleting the row, so a value that has stopped being true is still there with the span it was true for. It inherits the arity question and does not answer it — closing is replacing and not closing is coexisting — and it closes, because a frame that never closed would have nothing closed and the separation of what is true now from what was ever true would not exist. It answers as of the moment it is asked.

**What these figures say.**

- **The interval store buys history and the workload never asks for it.** Every question a checkpoint asks is about the present, so what this structure does that no other arm can — answer what was true in an earlier year — is unmeasured here. Its recall and stale figures are `attribute-single`'s and its extra rows are the price of a capability this workload does not exercise. That is a finding about the instrument: the seam has no way to ask about the past because the workload never asks one.

## `interval-declared` — spans, with arity stated by the write

The interval store taking the declaration instead of deciding for itself, so a wrongly-declared replacement closes a span rather than removing a row and the value is still there. Like `attribute-declared` it is a ceiling rather than a candidate: the write hands it the decision every self-deciding structure has to make.

Whether keeping the value changes what can be answered is §Whether a write need destroy's question, and that section reports it.

## `log` — events, with the memory computed from them

Every observation is an event, appended and never changed; the memory is not stored but computed from the log when it is asked for. Its arity decision lives in the projection, which makes it the only structure here where that decision can be taken again: no event is lost, so a different projection over the same events is a different memory with nothing re-observed. `harness/log_test.go` runs one log through both projections and gets both memories, which is what that claim rests on.

**What these figures say.** The projection it runs takes the newest value at an address, which is `attribute-single`'s and `interval`'s decision in a third store; §What the comparison shows carries what follows from that and what separates the three.

## `generative-agents` — a scored memory stream that never forgets

Park et al. 2023, *Generative Agents: Interactive Simulacra of Human Behavior* (arXiv:2304.03442, UIST 2023). Every observation enters a stream as a record and nothing ever leaves it. A read scores every record on three terms — recency, an exponential decay on the hours since the record was last accessed; importance, the poignancy a model assigns at write time; relevance, the cosine between the record and the query — min-max normalizes each, sums them at equal weight, and fills the context from the top of that ranking.

**What it needed that the seam withholds.** Three things, and each is a substitution rather than a widening. A query, taken from the turn's text, which is what the shipped frame does. A limit, which the seam names nowhere: it takes the shipped recall budget, so it fills the same context `document` and `shipped` fill and a difference between them is not a difference of budget. And a model at write time to score importance, substituted by the salience the shipped writeback would have carried — the two constants §The run reports — so both arms bracket it exactly as they bracket everything else a writeback decides. That substitution takes two values rather than the paper's ten, so after min-max scaling it is a flag saying whether the turn asserted anything: coarser than a poignancy score and more separated than one. In the `text` arm it takes one value and the importance term contributes nothing at all.

**What these figures say.**

- **It answers about a tenth of what it is asked, and the reason is what it stores rather than how it ranks.** A decade of turns is a store of thousands, most of them small talk, and the design keeps all of it. Its first year is its best and its recall falls year on year as the store grows, which is the shape of a ranking problem getting harder rather than a memory decaying — nothing is ever removed. Against it, every structure that stores what a turn asserted rather than the turn answers most of what it is asked at every year, out of a store an order of magnitude smaller.
- **It is the one design here whose failure is not visible in the stale figure either.** Its stale figure is low, and low for the wrong reason: it holds every superseded value it ever saw and its ranking mostly fails to surface those too. A structure that answers little returns little that is wrong, and neither number is a credit.
- **`replaced` is the class it never answers.** It scores nothing on that class at the last year, where it scores something on every other. A thread whose value has been overwritten twice has three sentences in the store saying different things about one attribute, and a ranking with no notion of which of them is current has no way to prefer the last. That is the arity question arriving at a design that does not address facts: it does not decide arity wrongly, it has no place to decide it.
- **It is the first structure in this record with a frozen share, and it is most of the store.** §What a read cannot reach reports it: four fifths of the planted values it holds have never once reached a context, and hundreds of those are still asked for later. A store that outgrows what its reads can carry is the case the earlier round predicted and could not produce, and keeping every observation produces it.

## `reflection` — the same stream, with a synthesis written back into it

The second mechanism of the same paper. When the importance summed since the last reflection passes 150, the design writes a higher-level record over the last hundred, and that record is retrievable like any other.

**What it needed that the seam withholds.** A model to write the synthesis. There is none, so the synthesis here is the window's records at the higher importance, joined into one — the operation a summarizer performs and not the inference one does. What this row bounds is what aggregating observations into one retrievable object buys; it does not measure what a model's insight would add, and no figure below should be read as though it did. Taking the whole window instead would fill the synthesis with small talk and measure the noise; taking the records the write marked is the closest a non-inferring operation comes to what a model would have picked out of the window.

One consequence of that is worth stating before its rows are read: in the `text` arm no write marks anything, one importance is all there is, and the synthesis therefore selects nothing — so this design posts `generative-agents`'s rows there. That is a property of the substitution and not of the design, and it is the only place in this record where two rows are equal for a reason that is not a result.

Two properties keep the substitution from eating the structure: a reflection is never read into a later reflection, and no reflection outgrows what a context holds. `harness/stream_test.go` holds both, because without them a synthesis of syntheses doubles at every trigger and the structure stops being a memory.

**What these figures say.**

- **Aggregating observations into a retrievable object buys nothing this run can separate.** Its recall stands a little above `generative-agents`' at the last year and the two intervals overlap, on every class and on the total. What it does buy is measured and negative: a third again as much content held, two hundred more records, and the same stale figure.
- **What it does move is what a read can reach, and not by enough.** Its frozen share is below `generative-agents`' at every year — a synthesis is one record carrying many values, so returning it reaches values a per-record ranking never surfaced — and it is still seven tenths of what the store holds. Aggregation makes a store of everything slightly more reachable and leaves it unreachable.
- **`memory-architecture/` measured this mechanism winning and this workload does not reproduce that.** That programme found reflection ahead on recall and precision on an eight-category workload. The two runs share no workload, no metric and no instrument, so this is not a contradiction of that result; what it says is that on a decade of one user's threads, with the synthesis rendered as a summarizer rather than an inference, the mechanism does not separate from the stream it is built on.

## `memorybank` — the stream under a forgetting curve

Zhong et al. 2023, *MemoryBank: Enhancing Large Language Models with Long-Term Memory* (arXiv:2305.10250, AAAI 2024). The same stream, with an Ebbinghaus retention of `exp(-t/S)` in the days `t` since a record was last recalled over the strength `S` its rehearsals have built. A retrieval rehearses what it returns — strength up by one, elapsed days back to zero — and a record whose retention falls under the threshold is forgotten.

**What it needed that the seam withholds.** The threshold. The paper states the curve and not a cut, so the cut is the harness's and is named where the rule reads it: one half-life, so a record goes once the days since a read last returned it pass its strength.

**What these figures say.**

- **It scores the floor exactly.** Its recall at the last year is `document`'s, to the third decimal and with the same interval, and it answers no class at all except the one asserted in the year it is asked. A published design and the trivial structure this record keeps as the floor are indistinguishable here.
- **The reason is the span and not the mechanism.** The curve is written in days, because it models a person over days, and a strength of one gives a record about a day. A checkpoint stands a year after the last one, so a record has to be rehearsed hundreds of times to survive between two questions and nothing in the workload rehearses it that often. The structure holds a couple of dozen records at every checkpoint for the whole decade: it is a recency window with an exponential in front of it.
- **The idea inside it is one this record has already found working, in another form.** Rehearsal on retrieval is what §What decides the eviction measures as least-recently-read, and that rule keeps `carried` intact where an age-ordered rule loses all of it. What fails here is the time constant, not the signal. A decade-scale version of this design would need the curve rescaled to the span it runs over, and rescaling it is choosing a number the paper does not supply.
- **It is the only structure here that forgets enough to have nothing frozen and nothing to answer with.** Its frozen share is zero at every year for the same reason `document`'s is: a store small enough for every read to cycle through holds nothing a read has not reached.

## `mem0` — an operation stated by the write, applied to a memory found by similarity

Chhikara et al. 2025, *Mem0: Building Production-Ready AI Agents with Scalable Long-Term Memory* (arXiv:2504.19413). Two phases. A model reads the message and emits the facts it asserts; then, for each fact, the design retrieves the ten most similar memories it already holds and a model chooses among add, update, delete and no-op over them.

It is here because it is the published design closest to what §What would be chosen today recommends, and because it differs from that recommendation in exactly one decision. Both take the write operation from a model reading the sentence. This one then has to find which memory the operation applies to, and finds it by similarity; the addressed store computes the address and goes there. Running the two on one set of declarations separates identity from arity, which no run in this record had separated before.

**What it needed that the seam withholds.** A model in the write path, twice over. The first call is extraction, which the arms already bracket: under the oracle it is perfect and in the `text` arm there is none, so this design holds nothing there for the reason every addressed structure holds nothing there. The second call is the judgement — whether a candidate is the memory this fact updates — and nothing in the seam supplies it. The paper states no threshold, because the model decides. So two arms bracket it instead: `mem0` takes the nearest candidate whose similarity clears the constant this project's own store supersedes at, and `mem0-nearest` takes the nearest candidate whatever its similarity. The design sits between them at a point nothing measured here can locate, which is the shape of answer §The arity question gave `attribute-learned`. §What a similarity-located write has to separate is the evidence about where a working gate would have to sit.

Its read is handed the address in the arm that supplies one, rendered as the query string its retrieval takes, so it is given exactly what every addressed frame is given. What separates it from the addressed store is what it does with an address, not what it knows.

**What these figures say.**

- **The identity step almost never fires, and the design lands near the always-join end of the arity question.** Of the assertions the decade plants, the gate accepts a candidate on a small fraction; the rest are added beside what was already there. So the store ends the decade holding nearly every value ever written, answers nearly everything, and returns a superseded value on nine revised threads in ten. That is `attribute-multi`'s position on both figures, reached by a design that was trying to update.
- **Where it does fire, it lands on the wrong memory, and the failure shows up in a class nothing else fails.** It is short of a full answer on `carried` — a fact asserted once in the first year, never revised and never contradicted. No addressed structure in this record has ever lost one: a write goes to its own address, so a fact nobody wrote to again cannot be disturbed. Here a write meant for one address ends a value at another. That is the sharpest structural difference the round found, and it is not visible in a total: an addressed store's failures land on the classes the workload aimed at them, and a similarity-located store's land anywhere.
- **§What a similarity-located write has to separate says why, and says it without a threshold.** The value already at the address a write lands on is, on average, no nearer that write than the nearest value at some other address — the two figures' intervals overlap — and for nine assertions in ten the nearest value in the whole store is at another address entirely. There is no gate that separates them, so the two arms are not two settings of a knob that could be tuned between: one end never fires and reproduces always-join, the other fires every time and destroys the store, and nothing in between is better because there is nothing to be better about. This is `memory-retrieval/`'s refutation of similarity-based identity arriving in a second, structural form.
- **It reads its whole store to answer one question.** The units-read column puts it with `shipped`, `log` and `attribute-learned` in the family §What the comparison shows names as not surviving a decade, and its stored bytes are two orders of magnitude above an addressed store's because every memory carries the vector it is found by.

## `mem0-nearest` — the same design with the judgement removed

`mem0` with no gate: an update ends the nearest memory however far away it is. It is not a design anyone proposes and belongs in no ranking — it is the far end of the bracket the section above describes, and it is here for the reason `attribute-multi` is here.

**What these figures say.**

- **Removing the judgement destroys the store.** It ends the decade holding a tenth of what was written to it and answering under a fifth of what it is asked, and it answers `carried` on nothing at all: a fact asserted in the first year is ended by some later write about something else long before it is asked in the last. What it does answer is mostly the year's own assertions, which is what a store that has just been written looks like.
- **Its stale figure is zero, and that is the clearest case in this record of why recall is never reported alone.** It returns no superseded value because it has destroyed nearly everything, including the values that are still true. A column ranking on staleness alone would put this row at the top of the table.

## Method

Every memory experiment in this tree so far has compared variants inside one frame: a store of rows, an embedding-similarity retrieval step, a per-turn model decision, and a score that ranks rows. This one builds the instrument for comparing frames rather than variants, and measures the shipped design through it. It proposes no new design; the designs are separate issues.

Like `memory-retrieval/`, the shipped design is measured as it runs rather than as a reimplementation of it: the store, the hybrid search, the ranking function and the after-turn lifecycle are `internal/memory`'s own, called through `memory.NewService` and `internal/gateway`'s salience constants. Unlike `memory-retrieval/`, it is one implementation behind an interface rather than the thing the harness is wired to, so the next design is added without the harness changing.

Every figure in this record is produced by `harness/` from the committed results and written into this file between the generated markers. No figure in this record was typed by hand, which is why there is no register of claims here: the project owner has ruled that a record restating result figures should generate them instead. `harness/record_test.go` fails when this file and the committed results disagree.

## The seam

`harness/frame.go` declares it. A turn goes in; what the design would put in the model's context at any later turn comes out; what it holds can be asked for.

The interface returns text rather than rows. A design that keeps one rewritten document has no rows to return, and scoring on rows would settle the comparison before it ran. Nothing in the interface names a query, a limit, a score or a similarity, so a design that performs no retrieval answers by returning what it holds. The bounded-document frame exists to prove that is true rather than claimed: it keeps no store, no identity and no ranking, and sits behind the same interface as the shipped design.

**What the seam withholds is a claim about the comparison, not a limitation to work around.** A design needing a query, a limit or a score can take one and say where it came from — the ported designs take a query from the turn and a limit from the shipped recall budget, which is what keeps every context-filling structure filling the same context. A design whose distinguishing step is a thing the seam withholds cannot be ported without a substitute, and §Where the published designs came from names each such design and what it needed rather than widening the seam quietly.

What a design is told about a turn is the other half of the seam. A turn always carries its text. In one arm it also carries the assertion the generator planted, as a subject, an attribute and a value. That is there because with no model in the run, a design that addresses facts by subject and attribute would otherwise have to parse sentences, and the comparison would become a comparison of parsers. Holding extraction constant removes that variable.

The extracted form says what was asserted and nothing else. It does not say whether an attribute holds one value or several, and it does not say what an assertion replaces. Arity and supersession are the questions being asked; a design handed either has been handed the answer.

One more thing arrives in that arm and it is not extraction. `Declared` is the write operation — whether the value ends what is at its address or joins it — which is where this record's own arity finding puts it: nothing recovers from the triple what the sentence carried, so only a writeback that reads the sentence can state it. Every frame receives it and only the ceiling reads it, which is what makes that arm a ceiling rather than a design. `Fact` still says nothing about arity, so the question the other frames are asked is the question they were asked before.

A question is a turn too, and in the same arm it also arrives extracted: the address it is asking about, never the value. That is the read-side half of the same decision. A design that addresses facts has to turn a question into an address, and with no model in the run it would otherwise parse the question — which would put on the read side exactly the parser comparison that was removed from the write side. Every frame receives it and each decides what to do with it: the shipped frame ignores it and queries with the question's text, because its read path takes a task statement and giving it an address would stop it being the shipped design.

**What a question carries is an axis of its own, and tying it to the arm is what left one half of the standard unmeasured.** While the two moved together, an addressed structure had either a perfect extractor and a perfect question or neither, so nothing it scored said whether it could find. §Whether a structure can find what it holds runs this seam with the two separated — the arm decides what a write carries, the form decides what a question carries — and no frame was changed to allow it.

**The arms bracket the writeback, and the bracket is not optional.** The shipped store is curation-only: only the writeback block writes to it, and the writeback is a model decision (`docs/architecture/memory.md`). With no model in the run something has to stand in for it, and picking one stand-in would hide a choice that moves every figure. So both frames run in both arms, and the arms bracket the same thing for every design.

In the `oracle` arm a fact-carrying turn arrives already extracted, and the shipped frame writes it as the semantic add `internal/gateway/writeback.go` derives; every other turn is an episodic observation. In the `text` arm no turn carries an extracted fact, so every turn is an episodic observation. Neither frame is told which arm it is in — the arms differ only in what a turn carries, and a frame that reads only the text cannot tell them apart, which `harness/measure_test.go` checks.

**The `oracle` arm is a ceiling, not a measurement of the shipped design.** It hands the writeback a perfect extractor that never misses a fact and never invents one. No real writeback has come close: `memory-cycle/` measured zero voluntary writeback blocks in fifteen of fifteen responses, and #336 is open on it. A reader who takes the `oracle` row for what the shipped design scores is reading the baseline flattered.

## The workload

`harness/workload.go` generates it from a seed. The span, the session count, the turns per session and the thread count are the run's parameters, reported in the results table below rather than buried as constants: a later comparison cannot be read against a noise volume nobody chose.

Sessions are spread over the years with a jitter inside the gap between them; turns inside a session are minutes apart. Most turns carry no fact at all. Assertions are scattered among them, each in a turn of a session inside its own year.

A **thread** is one subject's value for one attribute over the whole span. Six classes differ only in how a thread behaves:

- `carried` — asserted once in the first year and never mentioned again. It must still be answerable in the last year.
- `replaced` — asserted, replaced, and replaced again. Only the latest value may come back.
- `reverted` — asserted, replaced, and then returned to the value it started with. What must come back is a value the thread already superseded once.
- `parallel` — two values of one attribute, both true, asserted years apart, neither contradicting the other.
- `refined` — one value, and then a narrower statement of the same attribute that does not contradict it. This is the case `memory-creation/`'s best-scoring structure beat with a per-case rule, which is why it is here.
- `fresh` — asserted in the year it is asked. The control that says whether a frame holds what it has just seen.

At the end of every year, every thread answerable that year is asked its attribute's question. The question names the subject and the attribute and never the value. What is then read off the answer is what §The structures compared reports: recall, stale returned and units read. A checkpoint reads a frame and never writes to it.

Every planted value is a hyphenated pair of words, and no pair is planted on more than one thread. A value is therefore a token that identifies the assertion carrying it, so crediting is exact by reading — the trap #371 had to add a word boundary for cannot arise. `harness/workload_test.go` checks the uniqueness, checks that no turn carrying no fact mentions a planted value, and checks each class's schedule; `harness/measure_test.go` checks the credit rule against the substring cases it has to reject.

`replaced` and `reverted` state the new value without repeating the old one. A correction that named what it corrects would plant the stale value in the transcript, and the stale figure would then be counting the workload's own words.

`parallel` and `refined` differ in wording and in nothing else: both plant two simultaneously-true values of one attribute years apart. A design that scores well on one and badly on the other is keying on phrasing.

**How the disqualification criterion is read off these numbers.** The project owner's standard is that a design needing code added to handle a particular case is disqualified rather than patched. A per-case rule is not visible in a total, which is how the best-scoring structure of the last round passed. It is visible in a profile: each class is reported at each year, and the two coexistence classes are the same case in different words. A structure whose score comes from a rule written for one case has an uneven profile across the classes, or a gap between `parallel` and `refined`, and the tables below are laid out so that both show.

## The arity question

Addressing a fact at `(subject, attribute)` settles identity and moves the problem rather than removing it. If an address holds one value, a write replaces, and two values that are true at once have nowhere to live. If it holds many, nothing is ever replaced, and the structure reproduces the baseline's failure by a different route. So the frame has to decide, and the seam withholds the answer.

**No rule was found that is worth believing in.** Four were considered and three were rejected before being built; what follows is what each would have read, and the table §What the addresses look like is where that reading was checked rather than assumed.

- **The interval between the writes to one address.** A window that keeps recent values and retires older ones. The table gives the interval between the last two writes on every class: the two classes whose earlier value must go and the two whose earlier value must stay have intervals whose confidence intervals all overlap. No window separates them. For a timing rule to work, a workload would have to be one in which correcting is prompt and accumulating is slow — where a replacement follows what it replaces sooner than a second simultaneously-true value follows the first — and this one is not.
- **The statement's length**, on the reasoning that an addition has to say what makes it different and so costs words. Tested against the workload's own sentences before it was built: for one attribute the replacement is the longer statement and for another it is the shorter. It would have scored by accident.
- **Similarity between the new statement and the one already at the address.** This puts back exactly what addressing removes, and `memory-retrieval/` measured that it cannot separate a correction from two facts that must both survive — on the shipped embedder the correction family's median sits below the coexisting family's minimum.
- **Arity as a property of the attribute, learned from the attribute's own addresses.** This one is built and measured, as `attribute-learned`, because it is the rule the question invites. It is one rule and it needs no clause per case. §`attribute-learned` states it and reports what it did.

**What separates the classes is the wording, and `attribute-cued` is that rule written down.** §`attribute-cued` states it, reports what it scored, and says why scoring best of every arm measured here is not enough.

That wording is the only thing separating these classes is a fact about the workload, and a fact about conversation. Nothing but the words distinguishes "she leads that project as well" from "she leads that project now", and no structure recovers from the triple what the sentence carried. The conclusion is not that language is a poor signal. It is that a rule enumerating language is a poor structure, and that arity belongs where the language is read — in the write operation — rather than in the store that receives it.

## What a wrong declaration costs

`attribute-declared` answers everything and returns nothing stale, and it was measured with a declaration that is never wrong. In production that declaration comes from a model reading a sentence, and models err. This section measures what the recommendation costs when it is wrong, whether the damage repairs itself, and where a real model sits.

**The two directions are never blended, here or in any table below.** A write that should have joined and arrives as a replacement ends a value that was still true: the information is gone. A write that should have replaced and arrives as a join leaves a value that has stopped being true beside the one that replaced it: the information is wrong but still there. One rate covering both would let a reader average a failure that destroys with one that accumulates, and the tables report each against its own denominator.

The corruption is a property of the run and not of any structure. It is applied where an arm hands a turn over, so no frame carries a clause for it and no frame can tell a corrupted declaration from an honest one — which is what a frame would face from a writeback that is sometimes wrong. Which writes are corrupted comes from each turn's own index, so a rate corrupts the same writes whatever order a run reaches them in.

**Only the declared-operation frame is swept, and only in the oracle arm.** It is the one structure that reads a declaration, so it is the only one a wrong declaration can reach; the text arm carries no declaration for anything to be wrong about.

**What the rate is a share of.** Each direction can only corrupt the writes that declare its operation, and the eligible counts are in the table. They are very different: most writes over a decade replace, and the ones that join are the two coexistence classes' second values alone. The join direction therefore has coarse resolution — a rate below one over its eligible count corrupts nothing, which is why its lowest rows show no errors and no damage rather than a small amount of both.

**A write to an empty address cannot be wrong.** The first assertion at an address is the same store either way it is declared, so its declaration is unfalsifiable. Most assertions over a decade are of that kind, which is why the model's rates below are reported against two denominators: every eligible write, which is what the sweep's rate is a share of, and the writes that land on an address already holding something, which are the ones a declaration has consequences for.

**Healing is measured, not asserted.** After a wrong declaration, the tables report the share of corrupted addresses answering correctly again at the last year and how many checkpoints each carried the damage. `harness/misdeclaration_test.go` asserts the asymmetry those columns show.

**What these figures say.**

- **The two error directions fail in opposite figures, and one of them is invisible in recall.** A wrong replacement takes recall down and never touches the stale figure; a wrong join leaves recall at its ceiling at every rate measured, up to and including corrupting half of every eligible write, and shows up only as staleness. A table ranking on recall alone would report the accumulating failure as no failure at all, which is why recall never appears in this record without the stale figure beside it.
- **One of them heals and the other does not, and that is the structural difference.** A wrong join is repaired by the next correct replacement at that address, so nearly all of them are gone by the last year and the ones that are not are addresses the workload never writes to again. A wrong replacement is repaired by nothing: it destroys a value that is still true, and nothing in the workload asserts that value again. Healing is a property of what a structure does next, not of what it holds, and addressing supplies it in one direction only.
- **What repairs the one and not the other is the same mechanism.** A replacement clears the address, so it clears a wrongly-kept value with it; nothing re-writes a wrongly-cleared one. Making a wrong replacement heal would take a record of what was cleared and a rule for putting it back — which is what the interval frame holds and what §`interval` says it holds it for, and a rule for when to put a value back is a rule about arity again.
- **A real model does not reach the ceiling, and the gap is one class.** Driven by a model's own declarations rather than by a rate, the frame answers every class at every year until the year the coexistence values arrive, and from then on loses only `refined`. `parallel` — the same requirement worded with a conjunction — stays at its ceiling for the whole decade. Every one of the model's destroying errors landed on the narrowing wording and none landed on the conjunction.
- **The model reproduces the cue list's failure, which says the failure is not the cue list's.** `attribute-cued` is disqualified because it enumerates the words that mark an addition, and it fails on `refined` and passes `parallel`. A model that enumerates nothing fails on the same class and passes the same class. What that says is not that the model is a cue list, but that a conjunction states arity and a qualifier only implies it — so an addition marked by implicature is hard to read rather than merely hard to list, and the disqualification stands against the rule and not against reading language.
- **Most of what a writeback declares cannot be wrong, and a rate that counts it says very little.** The overwhelming majority of assertions over a decade land on an address holding nothing, where replacing and joining are the same store. Counted over every eligible write the model's replacing errors look catastrophic; counted over the writes that land somewhere they are a fifth. The stale figure the frame actually posts follows the second and not the first.
- **The recommendation does not survive intact, and what it becomes is a trade rather than a ruin.** On this model's declarations the structure loses recall in one class permanently and returns a superseded value on about one revised thread in five, against a ceiling of neither. Set beside the arms that decide arity for themselves, it recovers recall that always-replacing gives up and pays for it in staleness that always-replacing does not have — and the staleness heals while the lost recall does not. Neither dominates, so "the write states arity" is a choice about which failure is cheaper rather than a structure that removes both.
- **On the same declarations the published design answers more than the recommendation does, and this is the round's one figure where it is ahead.** `mem0` posts a higher recall than `attribute-declared` and keeps `refined` at its ceiling where the addressed store loses a quarter of it. That is a real difference on a real figure and it is stated first, before what it costs.
- **What buys it is that the design's destroying error mostly fails to destroy.** The model's wrong replacements reach `mem0` as instructions to end a memory it then cannot find, so they add instead: nearly all of its wrongly-destroying writes heal and they carry their damage for a fraction of a checkpoint, where the same errors on the addressed store heal on nothing and carry it for half the span. The recall it is ahead on is bought by an identity step that usually misses, and the price is on the next line.
- **It pays for it with a superseded value on nearly every revised thread, against a fifth for the addressed store.** Its stale figure on the model's declarations is more than four times the addressed store's. So the two are not close: one loses a class and returns little that is wrong, the other keeps every class and returns a great deal that is wrong. That is the same trade §Whether a write need destroy already measured between always-replacing and always-joining, and `mem0` sits at the joining end of it — which is where a design whose updates rarely land has to sit.

## Whether a write need destroy

The two failures are not symmetric and the record already says why: a replacement clears the address, so it clears a wrongly-kept value with it, and nothing re-writes a wrongly-cleared one. That leaves one question, and it is the last one before a structure can be chosen — can a structure be built in which no write destroys, and what does it cost?

Three things are measured, all on the declarations already committed.

**Erring toward the cheaper failure.** A share of the writes that declare a replacement is resolved to a join instead, from taking every declaration as it comes to joining whatever the declaration says. The two ends are structures anyone could build; every point between them is a share of writes chosen by nothing, so the interior is a measuring instrument and not a design. What it is for is the shape of the trade, which is what a chooser needs and what this record did not have.

**A store where replacement closes a span rather than deleting.** `interval-declared` is run beside `attribute-declared` on the same declarations, and the question is whether keeping the value changes what can be answered.

**Whether the model is in doubt where it is wrong.** A bias needs something to bias on, and the candidate that does not ask the model for a number is asking it more than once and treating the samples disagreeing as the doubt. Every assertion whose declaration has a consequence was asked again, several times, at a temperature that lets the answer vary. Whether disagreement tracks error at all is the figure that decides whether any doubt-reading rule is possible, and it is reported before the rule built on it.

`harness/no_destroy_test.go` asserts each of these: that the interval store holds what a wrong declaration cleared and still cannot answer with it, that the bias's far end is what keeping every value already does, that both declaration-reading stores answer identically, and that a split resolves to a join.

**What these figures say.**

- **Nothing recovers a wrongly-cleared value, and the store that keeps it proves the point rather than solving it.** `interval-declared` and `attribute-declared` on the same declarations answer identically at every year and every class, while the interval store holds a quarter again as many units — every value a wrong declaration cleared, sitting in a closed span. A question about the present does not return a closed span, so the information is retained and unreachable. Keeping it is not the same as recovering it, and the gap between those two is where this round ends.
- **Reaching it would take a rule for when to reopen a closed span, which is the arity question again.** The two total policies are the ends already measured: reopen nothing and the answer is the addressed store's; reopen everything and it is what keeping every value scores, staleness at its ceiling. Anything between them decides, after the fact, whether a particular closure was a replacement or a join — which is the decision the whole programme has been trying to source, arriving one step later. The loop closes without an answer, so the destroying error is irreducible on the evidence here and a structure has to be chosen knowing it.
- **Erring toward join blindly is a bad trade, and the exchange rate is the reason.** Joining every declared replacement recovers every destroying error and puts staleness at its ceiling; the curve between is close to linear. The recall it buys is small because the destroying errors are few, and the staleness it pays is large because the correct replacements are many — so a blind bias is overwhelmingly likely to spoil a write that was right. A policy that cannot tell which declarations to doubt should not be biased at all.
- **The model is in doubt about four times more often where it is wrong, and it is not enough.** Asked repeatedly, its answers split far more often on the assertions it gets wrong than on the ones it gets right, so disagreement is a real signal and not noise. But most of its wrong answers are given again every time it is asked: the error is settled, not uncertain. Resolving every split to a join recovers well under half the destroying errors and wrongly joins more correct replacements than it recovers.
- **The one bias that needs no number from the model is measurable, better than blind, and still does not close the gap.** Asking three times and joining where the samples split beats blind biasing by a wide margin on what it buys per point of staleness. It costs three model calls where there was one, carries a sample count as a knob, and leaves the coexistence class short of where a correct declaration would put it. It is reported as what it is rather than recommended.
- **The two stores that read a declaration are separated by what they hold and what they read, and by nothing a reader can ask about.** That is the same result #457 found among three stores under one self-deciding rule, holding now under a declaration that is sometimes wrong. Whatever is chosen, it is not chosen on what the memory answers.
